Denote by 𝒫 log \mathcal{P}_{\log} the set of all non-constant Pick functions f f whose logarithmic derivatives f ′ / f f^{\,\prime}/f also belong to the Pick class. Let 𝒰 ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{U}(\Lambda) be the family of functions z ⋅ f ⁡ ( z ) z\cdot f(z) , where f ∈ 𝒫 log f\in\mathcal{P}_{\log} and f f is holomorphic on Λ := ℂ ∖ [ 1 , + ∞ ) \Lambda:={\mathbb{C}}\setminus[1,+\infty) . Important examples of functions in 𝒰 ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{U}(\Lambda) are the classical polylogarithms L ​ i α ​ ( z ) Li_{\alpha}(z) : ⁣ = := ∑ k = 1 ∞ z k / k α \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}z^{k}/k^{\alpha} for α ≥ 0 \alpha\geq 0 , see [ 6 ] (2015). In this note we prove that every φ ∈ 𝒰 ⁡ ( Λ ) \varphi\in\mathcal{U}(\Lambda) is universally starlike, i.e., φ \varphi maps every circular domain in Λ \Lambda containing the origin one-to-one onto a starlike domain. Furthermore, we show that every non-constant function f ∈ 𝒫 log f\in\mathcal{P}_{\log} belongs to the Hardy space H p H_{p} on the upper half-plane for some constant p = p ⁡ ( f ) > 1 p=p(f)>1 , unless f f is proportional to some function ( a − z ) − θ (a-z)^{-\theta} with a ∈ ℝ a\in{\mathbb{R}} and 0 < θ ≤ 1 0<\theta\leq 1 . Finally we derive a necessary and sufficient condition on a real-valued function v v for which there exists f ∈ 𝒫 log f\in\mathcal{P}_{\log} such that v ⁡ ( x ) = lim ε ↓ 0 Im ​ f ​ ( x + i ​ ε ) v(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm{Im}}f(x+i\varepsilon) for almost all x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} . Introduction 1.1. Universally starlike functions Universally starlike functions have been introduced in [ 37 ] , [ 36 ] as analytic functions in the slit domain Λ := ℂ ∖ [ 1 , ∞ ) \Lambda:={\mathbb{C}}\setminus[1,\infty) satisfying the following conditions: (i) f ⁡ ( 0 ) = f ′ ​ ( 0 ) − 1 = 0 f(0)=f^{\prime}(0)-1=0 , (ii) f f is univalent in Λ \Lambda and maps any circular domain ℂ ⊂ Λ {\mathbb{C}}\subset\Lambda containing the origin onto a domain starlike with respect to the origin. In some subsequent papers this notion has been extended to other domains and classes of so-called (universally) prestarlike functions in Λ \Lambda of order α < 1 \alpha<1 have been introduced. The universally starlike functions correspond to the case α = 1 / 2 \alpha=1/2 and some interesting general properties and representations of those functions have been discovered. In particular, it turns out that these functions are necessarily Pick functions (in Λ \Lambda ), and can be represented in terms of specific probability measures on the interval [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] (see below). The main goal of this paper is to provide a complete characterization of the universally starlike functions, relying on the theory of Pick functions. The paper also contains many other results, which are interesting by themselves, regarding the Hardy classes H p H_{p} of the upper half-plane. In 2009 St. Ruscheweyh, L. Salinas and T. Sugawa [ 37 , Cor.1, p.289] proved that Ψ \Psi is universally starlike if and only if there exists μ ∈ ℳ + ​ ( ℝ ) \mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}) such that μ ⁡ ( ℝ ) = 1 \mu({\mathbb{R}})=1 , supp ⁡ μ ⊆ [ 0 , 1 ] \supp\mu\subseteq[0,1] and Ψ ⁡ ( z ) z = exp ( ∫ [ 0 , 1 ] log 1 1 − t ​ z d μ ( t ) ) , z ∈ ℂ ∖ [ 1 , + ∞ ) , \frac{\Psi(z)}{z}=\exp\left(\int_{[0,1]}\log\frac{1}{1-tz}\,d\mu(t)\right)\,,\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus[1,+\infty)\,, (1.1) where the principal branch of the logarithm is taken, ℳ + ​ ( ℝ ) \mathcal{M}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}) denotes the cone of all non-negative Borel measures on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} which are finite on compact sets, and supp ⁡ μ := { x ∈ ℝ ∣ μ ⁡ ( ( , , , ) ) > 0 ​ ∀ ε > 0 } . \supp\mu:=\left\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}\mid\mu\big((x-\varepsilon,\,x+\varepsilon)\big)>0\ \forall\varepsilon>0\right\}\,. Let 𝒰 ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathcal{U}(\Lambda) be the family of all functions z ⋅ f ⁡ ( z ) z\cdot f(z) , where f f is holomorphic on Λ := ℂ ∖ [ 1 , + ∞ ) \Lambda:={\mathbb{C}}\setminus[1,+\infty) and f f is a non-constant Pick function whose logarithmic derivative f ′ / f f^{\,\prime}/f also belong to the Pick class. One of the main results of the present paper is that every function φ ∈ 𝒰 ⁡ ( Λ ) \varphi\in\mathcal{U}(\Lambda) is universally starlike, i.e., φ \varphi maps every circular domain in Λ \Lambda containing the origin one-to-one onto a starlike domain. Let Hol ⁡ ( D ) \hol(D) denote the set of all holomorphic functions in D ⊂ ℂ D\subset{\mathbb{C}} , C ⁡ ( ℝ ) C({\mathbb{R}}) the set of all functions continuous on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} , ℬ ⁡ ( ℝ ) {\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{R}}) the family of all Borel subsets of ℝ {\mathbb{R}} , ℍ := { z ∈ ℂ ∣ Im ⁡ z > 0 } {\mathbb{H}}:=\left\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\IM z>0\right\} and 𝔻 := { z ∈ ℂ ∣ | z | < 1 } {\mathbb{D}}:=\left\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid|z|<1\right\} . For any 0 < p < + ∞ 0<p<+\infty we consider the Hardy spaces on the unit disk [ 24 , p.68] and on the upper half plane [ 11 , p. 168] , [ 24 , p. 112] as well H p ​ ( 𝔻 ) \displaystyle H_{p}({\mathbb{D}}) : = { f ∈ Hol ⁡ ( 𝔻 ) ∣ ‖ f ‖ H p ​ ( 𝔻 ) max ⁡ { 1 , p } := sup 0 ≤ r < 1 ∫ − π π | f ⁡ ( re i ​ θ ) | p ​ 𝑑 θ < + ∞ } , \displaystyle:=\left\{f\in\hol({\mathbb{D}})\mid\|f\|_{H_{p}({\mathbb{D}})}^{\max\{1,p\}}:=\sup\limits_{0\leq r<1}\int_{-\pi}^{\,\pi}\left|f(re^{i\theta})\right|^{p}\,d\theta<+\infty\right\}\,, H p ​ ( ℍ ) \displaystyle H_{p}({\mathbb{H}}) : = { f ∈ Hol ⁡ ( ℍ ) ∣ f 𝔻 ​ ( w ) := f ⁡ ( i ⁡ ( 1 − w ) 1 + w ) ∈ H p ​ ( 𝔻 ) } , \displaystyle:=\left\{f\in\hol({\mathbb{H}})\mid f_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w):=f\left(\frac{i(1-w)}{1+w}\right)\in H_{p}({\mathbb{D}})\right\}\,, ℋ p ​ ( ℍ ) \displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{p}({\mathbb{H}}) : = { f ∈ Hol ⁡ ( ℍ ) ∣ sup y > 0 ∫ ℝ | f ⁡ ( x + iy ) | p ​ dx < + ∞ } ⊂ H p ​ ( ℍ ) . \displaystyle:=\left\{f\in\hol({\mathbb{H}})\mid\sup\limits_{y>0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|f(x+iy)|^{p}\,dx<+\infty\right\}\subset H_{p}({\mathbb{H}})\,. We also deal with the real space L ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) L_{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}) consisting of all Borel measurable real-valued functions f f defined on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} such that ‖ f ‖ L ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) := inf { a > 0 ∣ m ⁡ ( { x ∈ ℝ ∣ | f ⁡ ( x ) | > a } ) = 0 } < + ∞ \|f\|_{L_{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})}:=\inf\left\{a>0\mid m\big(\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}\mid|f(x)|>a\}\big)=0\right\}<+\infty , where it is assumed that inf ∅ := + ∞ \inf\emptyset:=+\infty and m m denotes the Lebesgue measure on the real line. Recall that two functions in L ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) L_{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}) are identified whenever they are equal almost everywhere with respect to the Lebesgue measure m m (in short: m m -a.e. or just ’almost everywhere’). Let Ω ⊂ ℂ \Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}} be a domain containing the origin, Hol 1 ⁡ ( Ω ) \hol_{1}(\Omega) denote the class of functions in Hol ⁡ ( Ω ) \hol(\Omega) normalized by f ⁡ ( 0 ) = f ′ ​ ( 0 ) − 1 = 0 f(0)=f^{\prime}(0)-1=0 and Hol 0 ⁡ ( Ω ) \hol_{0}(\Omega) be the set of functions f ∈ Hol ⁡ ( Ω ) f\in\hol(\Omega) with f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 1 f(0)=1 . A domain is referred to as circular when it is a disk or a half plane. A domain Ω \Omega is called a starlike with respect to the origin if a straight line segment connecting an arbitrary point in it with the origin is contained in Ω \Omega . A function Ψ \Psi is called universally starlike (with respect to the origin) if Ψ ∈ Hol 1 ( ℂ ∖ [ 1 , + ∞ ) ) \Psi\in\hol_{1}({\mathbb{C}}\setminus[1,+\infty)) and every circular domain in ℂ ∖ [ 1 , + ∞ ) {\mathbb{C}}\setminus[1,+\infty) containing the origin is mapped by Ψ \Psi one-to-one onto a starlike domain. 1.2. Pick functions Let 𝒫 {\mathcal{P}} denote the set of Pick functions, i.e. the set of holomorphic functions Φ \Phi on ℍ {\mathbb{H}} with non-negative imaginary part, extended to − ℍ -{\mathbb{H}} by means of Φ ⁡ ( z ) = Φ ⁡ ( z ¯ ) ¯ \Phi(z)=\overline{\Phi(\overline{z})} for z ∈ − ℍ z\in-{\mathbb{H}} . Thus, 𝒫 := { Φ ∈ Hol ( ℂ ∖ ℝ ) | Im ⁡ Φ ⁡ ( z ) Im ⁡ z ≥ 0 , z ∈ ℂ ∖ ℝ ; Φ ( z ) = Φ ⁡ ( z ¯ ) ¯ , z ∈ − ℍ } . \mathcal{P}:=\left\{\Phi\in\hol({\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}})\ \left|\quad\frac{\IM\Phi(z)}{\IM z}\geq 0\,,\ z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}\,;\ \Phi(z)=\overline{\Phi(\overline{z})}\,,\ z\in-{\mathbb{H}}\ \right.\right\}\,. (1.2) It is known (see [ 8 , p.31] ) that every Φ ∈ 𝒫 \Phi\in\mathcal{P} is either a real constant or Im ⁡ Φ ⁡ ( z ) > 0 \IM\Phi(z)>0 for all z ∈ ℍ z\in{\mathbb{H}} . Each function in the class 𝒫 \mathcal{P} has a unique canonical representation of the form (see [ 10 , Th.1, p.20] , [ 39 , Lem.2.1, p.23] ) Φ ⁡ ( z ) = α ​ z + β + ∫ − ∞ + ∞ ( 1 t − z − t 1 + t 2 ) ​ 𝑑 σ ​ ( t ) , z ∈ ℂ ∖ ℝ , \Phi(z)=\alpha z+\beta+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{t-z}-\frac{t}{1+t^{2}}\right)\,d\sigma(t)\,,\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}\,, (1.3) where α ≥ 0 \alpha\geq 0 , β ∈ ℝ \beta\in{\mathbb{R}} and σ ∈ ℳ + ​ ( ℝ ) \sigma\in\mathcal{M}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}) for which ∫ ℝ ( 1 + t 2 ) − 1 ​ 𝑑 σ ​ ( t ) \int_{{\mathbb{R}}}(1+t^{2})^{-1}\,d\sigma(t) is finite. Conversely, any function of the form ( 1.3 ) is in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . It has been proved in [ 10 , Lem.2, p.26] that Φ ∈ 𝒫 ∩ Hol ( ( a , b ) ) ⇔ supp σ ⊂ ℝ ∖ ( a , b ) , \Phi\in\mathcal{P}\cap\hol\big(\,(a,b)\,\big)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\supp\sigma\subset{\mathbb{R}}\setminus(a,b)\,, (1.4) where a , b ∈ ℝ a,b\in{\mathbb{R}} , a < b a<b and σ \sigma is a measure corresponding to Φ \Phi by ( 1.3 ). For arbitrary Φ ∈ 𝒫 ∖ { 0 } \Phi\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{0\} we have log ⁡ Φ ∈ 𝒫 \log\Phi\in\mathcal{P} and any function f ∈ log ⁡ 𝒫 := { log ⁡ Φ ∣ Φ ∈ 𝒫 ∖ { 0 } } ⊂ 𝒫 f\in\log\mathcal{P}:=\big\{\log\Phi\mid\Phi\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{0\}\big\}\subset\mathcal{P} can be uniquely represented in the form (see [ 10 , p.27] ) f ⁡ ( z ) = β + ∫ − ∞ + ∞ ( 1 t − z − t 1 + t 2 ) ​ ρ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t , z ∈ ℂ ∖ ℝ , f(z)=\beta+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{t-z}-\frac{t}{1+t^{2}}\right)\,\rho(t)\,dt\,,\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}\,, (1.5) where β ∈ ℝ \beta\in{\mathbb{R}} and ρ ∈ L ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) \rho\in L_{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}) satisfies 0 ≤ ρ ⁡ ( x ) ≤ 1 0\leq\rho(x)\leq 1 for almost all x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} with respect to the Lebesgue measure (in short: for almost all ). Conversely, any function of the form ( 1.5 ) is in log ⁡ 𝒫 \log\mathcal{P} . 1.3. Boundary values of Pick functions For arbitrary Φ ∈ 𝒫 ∖ { 0 } \Phi\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{0\} we denote f := log ⁡ Φ ∈ log ⁡ 𝒫 f:=\log\Phi\in\log\mathcal{P} and replace the upper half plane by the unit disk with the help of the change of variables Φ 𝔻 ​ ( w ) := Φ ⁡ ( i ⁡ ( 1 − w ) 1 + w ) , f 𝔻 ​ ( w ) = log ⁡ Φ 𝔻 ​ ( w ) , w ∈ 𝔻 . \Phi_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w):=\Phi\left(\frac{i(1-w)}{1+w}\right)\,,\quad f_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w)=\log\Phi_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w)\,,\quad w\in{\mathbb{D}}\,. Then the formula ( 1.5 ) can be rewritten as follows (cp. [ 24 , p.76] ) f 𝔻 ​ ( w ) = β + i 2 ​ ∫ − π π e i ​ θ + w e i ​ θ − w ​ ρ ​ ( tan ⁡ θ 2 ) ​ 𝑑 θ . f_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w)=\beta+\frac{i}{2}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{\,e^{i\theta}+w\,}{e^{i\theta}-w}\ \rho\left(\tan\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\;d\theta\,. (1.6) In view of A.E. Plessner’s theorem [ 30 , p.17] (see [ 42 , Ex.24, p.245] , [ 24 , p.58] ), f 𝔻 ∈ Hol ⁡ ( 𝔻 ) f_{{\mathbb{D}}}\in\hol({\mathbb{D}}) and Im ⁡ f 𝔻 ≥ 0 \IM f_{{\mathbb{D}}}\geq 0 in 𝔻 {\mathbb{D}} imply that for almost all θ ∈ [ − π , π ] \theta\in[-\pi,\pi] there exists a finite limit f 𝔻 ​ ( e i ​ θ ) f_{{\mathbb{D}}}(e^{i\theta}) of f 𝔻 ​ ( w ) f_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w) as w ∈ 𝔻 w\in{\mathbb{D}} tends to e i ​ θ e^{i\theta} non-tangentially (in short: w ​ ⟶ ∠ ​ e i ​ θ w\overset{\angle}{\longrightarrow}e^{i\theta} ) [ 24 , p.11, 15] . Thus, for almost all x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} there exists the (non-tangential) finite boundary value f ⁡ ( x + i ​ 0 ) := lim ℍ ∋ z ​ → ∠ ​ x f ⁡ ( z ) f(x+i0):=\lim\limits_{{{\mathbb{H}}}\ni z\overset{\angle}{\rightarrow}x}f(z) and since f ⁡ ( tan ⁡ θ 2 + i ​ 0 ) = f 𝔻 ​ ( e i ​ θ ) f(\tan\frac{\theta}{2}+i\,0)=f_{{\mathbb{D}}}(e^{i\theta}) , θ ∈ ( − π , π ) \theta\in(-\pi,\pi) , in view of Fatou’s [ 24 , p.11] and Titchmarsh’s [ 24 , p.20] theorems we have f ⁡ ( tan ⁡ θ 2 + i ​ 0 ) = i ​ π ​ ρ ​ ( tan ⁡ θ 2 ) + β + lim 0 < ε → 0 ∫ ε π ρ ⁡ ( tan ⁡ ( θ + t ) 2 ) − ρ ⁡ ( tan ⁡ ( θ − t ) 2 ) 2 ​ tan ⁡ t 2 ​ 𝑑 t f\left(\tan{{\mbox{\large{$\frac{\theta}{\vphantom{A^{A^{a}}}2}$}}}}+i\,0\right)=i\pi\rho\left(\tan{{\mbox{\large{$\frac{\theta}{\vphantom{A^{A^{a}}}2}$}}}}\right)+\beta+\lim_{0<\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\pi}\frac{\rho\left(\tan{{\mbox{\large{$\frac{(\theta+t)}{\vphantom{A^{A^{a}}}2}$}}}}\right)-\rho\left(\tan{{\mbox{\large{$\frac{(\theta-t)}{\vphantom{A^{A^{a}}}2}$}}}}\right)}{2\tan{{\mbox{\large{$\frac{t}{\vphantom{A^{A^{a}}}2}$}}}}}\;dt (1.7) for almost all θ ∈ ( − π , π ) \theta\in(-\pi,\pi) . For the corresponding x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} the limit Φ ⁡ ( x + i ​ 0 ) := lim ℍ ∋ z ​ → ∠ ​ x Φ ⁡ ( z ) = lim ℍ ∋ z ​ → ∠ ​ x e f ⁡ ( z ) = e f ⁡ ( x + i ​ 0 ) \Phi(x+i0):=\lim_{{{\mathbb{H}}}\ni z\overset{\angle}{\rightarrow}x}\Phi(z)=\lim_{{\mathbb{H}}\ni z\overset{\angle}{\rightarrow}x}e^{f(z)}=e^{f(x+i0)} exists and is finite. Moreover, as straightforward consequences of well-known results in the theory of H p H_{p} -spaces, it is established in subsection 3.1 below that (cp. [ 15 , Lem. 6.2, p. 255] ) ∫ ℝ | Φ ⁡ ( x + i ​ 0 ) | ± δ 1 + x 2 ​ 𝑑 x < + ∞ , \displaystyle\int\nolimits_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{|\Phi(x+i0)|^{{{\mbox{\footnotesize{$\pm\delta$}}}}}}{1+x^{2}}\,dx<+\infty\,, δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) , \displaystyle\quad\delta\in(0,1)\,, Φ ∈ 𝒫 ∖ { 0 } , \displaystyle\quad\Phi\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{0\}\,, (1.8) ∫ ℝ | f ⁡ ( x + i ​ 0 ) | p + e δ ​ | f ⁡ ( x + i ​ 0 ) | 1 + x 2 ​ 𝑑 x < + ∞ , \displaystyle\int\nolimits_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{|f(x+i0)|^{{{\mbox{\footnotesize{$p$}}}}}+e^{\,{{\mbox{\footnotesize{$\delta|f(x+i0)|$}}}}}}{1+x^{2}}\;dx<+\infty\,, δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) , \displaystyle\quad\delta\in(0,1)\,, p > 0 , f ∈ log ⁡ 𝒫 , \displaystyle\quad p>0\,,\quad f\in\log{\mathcal{P}}\,, (1.9) 𝒫 ⊂ H δ ​ ( ℍ ) , log ⁡ 𝒫 ⊂ H p ​ ( ℍ ) , \displaystyle\mathcal{P}\subset H_{\delta}({\mathbb{H}})\,,\quad\log{\mathcal{P}}\subset H_{p}({\mathbb{H}})\,, δ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) , \displaystyle\quad\delta\in(0,1)\,, p > 0 . \displaystyle\quad p>0\,. (1.10) It should be noted that inequality ( 1.9 ) cannot be improved to cover the case δ = 1 \delta=1 because Φ ⁡ ( z ) = z ∈ 𝒫 ∖ { 0 } \Phi(z)=z\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{0\} and f ⁡ ( z ) = log ⁡ z ∈ log ⁡ 𝒫 f(z)=\log z\in\log{\mathcal{P}} . Main Results In order to formulate the main theorems it is first necessary to introduce some notations for the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measures and integrals. 2.1. But, if μ \mu is finite, we additionally assume that μ ⁡ ( − ∞ ) = 0 \mu(-\infty)=0 , i.e. 2 ​ μ ​ ( x ) := μ ⁡ ( ( , , , ) ) + lim ε ↓ 0 μ ⁡ ( ( , , , ) ) , x ∈ ℝ . 2\mu(x):=\mu\big(\,(-\infty,\,x)\big)+\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mu\big(\,(-\infty,\,x+\varepsilon)\,\big)\,,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}\,. 2.2. The classes 𝒫 ∫ ∩ 𝒫 {\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\mathcal{P} and 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 {\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log{\mathcal{P}} We recall that any harmonic function in ℍ {\mathbb{H}} is analytic in ℍ {\mathbb{H}} (see [ 18 , Th.1.15, p.31] ) and, in particular, all its partial derivatives of any order are continuous functions in ℍ {\mathbb{H}} . The following assertion is of independent interest. Theorem 2.1 . Let V ⁡ ( x , y ) V(x,y) be a harmonic function in ℍ {{\mathbb{H}}} and V ⁡ ( x , y ) ≥ 0 , V x ​ ( x , y ) ≥ 0 , y > 0 , x ∈ ℝ . V(x,y)\geq 0\,,\quad V_{x}(x,y)\geq 0\,,\quad y>0\,,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}\,. (2.8) Then the following statements hold: 1 . There exist a finite constant α ≥ 0 \alpha\geq 0 and a non-negative non-decreasing function ν \nu on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} such that ∫ 1 + ∞ ν ⁡ ( t ) t 2 ​ 𝑑 t < + ∞ , \int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{\nu(t)}{t^{2}}\,dt<+\infty\,, (2.9) and V ⁡ ( x , y ) = α ​ y + ∫ − ∞ + ∞ y y 2 + ( x − t ) 2 ​ ν ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t , y > 0 , x ∈ ℝ . V(x,y)=\alpha y+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{y}{y^{2}+(x-t)^{2}}\;\nu(t)\,dt\,,\quad y>0\,,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}\,. (2.10) Conversely, any function of this form is harmonic in ℍ {{\mathbb{H}}} and satisfies conditions ( 2.8 ). The representation ( 2.10 ) is unique if we identify non-decreasing functions that are equal almost everywhere on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} (see ( 2.4 ), ( 2.2 )) . 2 . Either V x ​ ( x , y ) > 0 V_{x}(x,y)>0 or V x ​ ( x , y ) ≡ 0 V_{x}(x,y)\equiv 0 for any y > 0 y>0 , x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} , and the latter case holds if and only if the function ν \nu in ( 2.10 ) is a non-negative constant function ν ⁡ ( x ) ≡ a ≥ 0 \nu(x)\equiv a\geq 0 , or, what is the same, V ⁡ ( x , y ) = α ​ y + a ⋅ π V(x,y)=\alpha y+a\cdot\pi , y > 0 y>0 , x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} , with some α , a ≥ 0 \alpha,a\geq 0 . Since in all the statements below the subclasses of 𝒫 ∖ { 0 } \mathcal{P}\setminus\{0\} are considered, we introduce the following general notations. For arbitrary f ∈ 𝒫 ∖ { 0 } f\in{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\{0\} denote U f ​ ( x , y ) := Re ⁡ f ⁡ ( x + iy ) , V f ​ ( x , y ) := Im ⁡ f ⁡ ( x + iy ) , x ∈ ℝ , y > 0 . U^{f}(x,y):=\RE f(x+iy)\,,\quad V^{f}(x,y):=\IM f(x+iy)\,,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\quad y>0\,. Corollary 2.2 . f ∈ 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 ∩ Hol ⁡ ( ( − ∞ , 1 ) ) f\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P}\cap\hol((-\infty,1)) if and only if there exists some μ ∈ ℳ + ​ ( ℝ ) \mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}) with supp ⁡ μ ⊆ [ 0 , 1 ] \supp\mu\subseteq[0,1] and μ ⁡ ( ℝ ) = 1 \mu({\mathbb{R}})=1 , such that f ⁡ ( z ) = ∫ [ 0 , 1 ] log ⁡ 1 1 − t ​ z ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( t ) , z ∈ ℍ . f(z)=\int_{[0,1]}\log\frac{1}{1-tz}\,d\mu(t)\,,\quad z\in{\mathbb{H}}\,. According to the definition ( 1.16 ) and Theorem 2.3 a non-constant function f f belongs to the class 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 {\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P} if and only if for every x + i ​ y ∈ ℍ x+iy\in{\mathbb{H}} , 0 < V f ​ ( x , y ) < π 0<V^{f}(x,y)<\pi and V x f ​ ( x , y ) > 0 V_{x}^{f}(x,y)>0 . But it follows from V x f = − U y f V_{x}^{f}=-U_{y}^{f} that the latter inequality is equivalent to U y f ​ ( x , y ) < 0 U_{y}^{f}(x,y)<0 , x + i ​ y ∈ ℍ x+iy\in{\mathbb{H}} . Thus, a non-constant function f f belongs to the class 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 {\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P} if and only if any one (and therefore all) of the following conditions hold: { ( a ) 0 < V ⁡ ( x 1 , y ) < V ⁡ ( x 2 , y ) < π , − ∞ < x 1 < x 2 < + ∞ , y > 0 , ( b ) U ( x , y 2 ) < U ( x , y 1 ) , 0 < V ( x , y ) < π , < y 1 < y 2 < + ∞ , y > 0 , x ∈ ℝ . \hskip-14.22636pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}(a)&0<V(x_{1},y)<V(x_{2},y)<\pi\,,&-\infty\!<\!x_{1}\!<\!x_{2}\!<\!+\infty\,,&y\!>\!0\,,\\[8.5359pt] (b)&U(x,y_{2})<U(x,y_{1})\,,\ 0<V(x,y)<\pi\,,&0\!<\!y_{1}\!<\!y_{2}\!<\!+\infty\,,&y\!>\!0\,,\ x\!\in\!{\mathbb{R}}\,.\end{array}\right.\hskip-4.26773pt (2.27) Indeed, the inequalities in the left-hand side of ( 2.27 ), (a) and (b), follow obviously from the inequalities V x f ​ ( x , y ) > 0 V_{x}^{f}(x,y)>0 and U y f ​ ( x , y ) < 0 U_{y}^{f}(x,y)<0 , respectively. Conversely, ( 2.27 ), (a) and (b), imply that the harmonic functions V x f ​ ( x , y ) V_{x}^{f}(x,y) and − U y f ​ ( x , y ) -U_{y}^{f}(x,y) are non-constant and non-negative, which means that they are strictly positive in ℍ {\mathbb{H}} in view of the mean value property of harmonic functions [ 18 , (1.5.4), p.34] . An exceptional position in the class 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 {\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P} is played only by those functions whose measure ν \nu given by ( 2.22 ) have a support consisting of at most one point. If ν \nu in ( 2.21 ) is a constant function (i.e., ν ⁡ ( ℝ ) = 0 \nu({\mathbb{R}})=0 for the corresponding measure ν \nu ) then by ( 2.22 ) f ≡ β + i ​ π ​ ν ​ ( − ∞ ) f\equiv\beta+i\pi\nu(-\infty) is a constant function as well, where ν ⁡ ( − ∞ ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \nu(-\infty)\in[0,1] by ( 2.23 ). If here we put θ = 0 \theta=0 , we get a constant function ν θ 1 , a , 0 ​ ( x ) ≡ θ 1 = ν ⁡ ( − ∞ ) \nu_{{{\mbox{\footnotesize{$\theta_{1},a,0$}}}}}(x)\equiv\theta_{1}=\nu(-\infty) . By ( 2.22 ), the measure ν θ 1 , a , θ \nu_{{{\mbox{\footnotesize{$\theta_{1},a,\theta$}}}}} corresponds to the function of the following form f θ 1 , a , θ ​ ( z ) = β + i ​ π ​ θ 1 ​ ( 1 − θ ) + θ ​ log ⁡ 1 + a 2 a − z , f_{{{\mbox{\footnotesize{$\theta_{1},a,\theta$}}}}}(z)=\beta+i\pi\theta_{1}\,(1-\theta)+\theta\,\log\frac{\sqrt{1+a^{2}}}{a-z}\,, and so we can define an exceptional class of functions in 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 {\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P} as follows Δ 𝒫 ∫ \displaystyle\Delta_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}} = { β + i π θ 1 ( 1 − θ ) + θ ⋅ log 1 + a 2 a − z | θ , θ 1 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] , a , β ∈ ℝ } \displaystyle=\left\{\beta+i\pi\theta_{1}\,(1-\theta)+\theta\cdot\log\frac{\sqrt{1+a^{2}}}{a-z}\ \,\Big|\ \,\theta,\theta_{1}\in[0,1]\,,\ a,\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}\,\right\} (2.28) = { β + i π θ 1 ⋅ ( 1 − θ ) + θ ⋅ log 1 a − z | θ , θ 1 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] , a , β ∈ ℝ } . \displaystyle=\left\{\beta+i\pi\theta_{1}\cdot(1-\theta)+\theta\cdot\log\frac{1}{a-z}\ \,\Big|\ \,\theta,\theta_{1}\in[0,1]\,,\ a,\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}\,\right\}\,. Our key result is contained in the following theorem. Theorem 2.4 . (2.31) 2.3. The class 𝒫 log \mathcal{P}_{\log} The next characteristic properties of the functions in the class 𝒫 log \mathcal{P}_{\log} follow immediately from ( 1.17 ) and ( 2.27 ). Theorem 2.5 . A non-constant function φ ∈ 𝒫 \varphi\in\mathcal{P} belongs to 𝒫 log \mathcal{P}_{\log} iff one of the following conditions holds: ( 1 ) \displaystyle(1) | φ ⁡ ( x + i ​ y 1 ) | > | φ ⁡ ( x + i ​ y 2 ) | , x ∈ ℝ , 0 < y 1 < y 2 < + ∞ ; \displaystyle\qquad\big|\varphi(x+iy_{1})\big|>\big|\varphi(x+iy_{2})\big|\,,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\quad 0<y_{1}<y_{2}<+\infty\,; ( 2 ) \displaystyle(2) arg ⁡ φ ⁡ ( x 1 + i ​ y ) < arg ⁡ φ ⁡ ( x 2 + i ​ y ) , y > 0 , − ∞ < x 1 < x 2 < + ∞ ; \displaystyle\qquad\arg\varphi(x_{1}+iy)<\arg\varphi(x_{2}+iy)\,,\quad y>0\,,\quad-\infty<x_{1}<x_{2}<+\infty\,; ( 3 ) \displaystyle(3) | U φ ​ ( x 1 , y ) U φ ​ ( x 2 , y ) V φ ​ ( x 1 , y ) V φ ​ ( x 2 , y ) | > 0 , y > 0 , − ∞ < x 1 < x 2 < + ∞ ; \displaystyle\qquad\begin{vmatrix}U^{\varphi}(x_{1},y)&U^{\varphi}(x_{2},y)\\ V^{\varphi}(x_{1},y)&V^{\varphi}(x_{2},y)\end{vmatrix}>0\,,\quad y>0\,,\quad-\infty<x_{1}<x_{2}<+\infty\,; ( 4 ) \displaystyle(4) U φ ​ ( x , y ) ​ U y φ ​ ( x , y ) + V φ ​ ( x , y ) ​ V y φ ​ ( x , y ) < 0 , x ∈ ℝ , y > 0 . \displaystyle\qquad U^{\varphi}(x,y)\,U_{y}^{\varphi}(x,y)+V^{\varphi}(x,y)\,V_{y}^{\varphi}(x,y)<0\,,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\quad y>0\,. (2.50) 3. Preliminary Results 3.1. Special properties of Pick functions Taking into account the special importance of the properties ( 1.8 )-( 1.10 ) and ( 1.19 ) for the theory of Pick functions, we give here independent simple proofs of these relations. For this purpose we recall a well-known result in the theory of H p H_{p} -spaces (see [ 24 , p.70] , [ 14 , Th.3.1, p.57] ) f ∈ H p ​ ( 𝔻 ) ⇒ ∃ lim r ↑ 1 f ⁡ ( r ​ e i ​ θ ) ∈ L p ​ ( [ − π , π ] , d ​ θ ) , 0 < p < + ∞ , f\in H_{p}({\mathbb{D}})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists\,\lim_{r\uparrow 1}f(re^{i\theta})\in L_{p}([-\pi,\pi],\,d\theta)\,,\quad 0<p<+\infty\,, (3.1) and observe that each Φ ∈ 𝒫 \Phi\in\mathcal{P} , Φ ≢ a \Phi\not\equiv a , a ∈ ℝ a\in{\mathbb{R}} , is an extension to Hol ⁡ ( ℂ ∖ ℝ ) \hol({\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}) of a unique function from the class 𝒫 + := { Φ ∈ Hol ⁡ ( ℍ ) ∣ Φ ⁡ ( ℍ ) ⊆ ℍ } , \mathcal{P}_{+}:=\left\{\Phi\in\hol({\mathbb{H}})\mid\Phi({\mathbb{H}})\subseteq{\mathbb{H}}\right\}\,, by means of the formula Φ ⁡ ( z ) = Φ ⁡ ( z ¯ ) ¯ \Phi(z)=\overline{\Phi(\overline{z})} , z ∈ − ℍ z\in-{\mathbb{H}} . 3.1.1. Proof of ( 1.8 ) - ( 1.10 ). Inequalities ( 1.8 ) and ( 1.9 ) are obviously valid for any constant functions. Thus, it is sufficient to prove them for all functions from 𝒫 + \mathcal{P}_{+} and log ⁡ 𝒫 + \log\mathcal{P}_{+} , respectively. Two fundamental properties of arbitrary function Φ ∈ 𝒫 + \Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{+} are: f := log ⁡ Φ ∈ 𝒫 + and Im ⁡ f = arg ⁡ Φ ∈ ( 0 , π ) . f:=\log\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{+}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\IM f=\arg\Phi\in(0,\pi)\,. Now, introducing the change of variables Φ 𝔻 ​ ( w ) := Φ ⁡ ( i ​ 1 − w 1 + w ) , f 𝔻 ​ ( w ) := f ⁡ ( i ​ 1 − w 1 + w ) = log ⁡ Φ 𝔻 ​ ( w ) , w ∈ 𝔻 , \Phi_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w):=\Phi\left(i\,\frac{1-w}{1+w}\right)\,,\qquad f_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w):=f\left(i\,\frac{1-w}{1+w}\right)=\log\Phi_{{\mathbb{D}}}(w)\,,\qquad w\in{\mathbb{D}}\,, we get Im ⁡ f 𝔻 = arg ⁡ Φ 𝔻 ∈ ( 0 , π ) , \IM f_{{\mathbb{D}}}=\arg\Phi_{{\mathbb{D}}}\in(0,\pi)\,, and so Φ 𝔻 \Phi_{{\mathbb{D}}} satisfies the conditions of a theorem of V. Smirnov (1928) [ 40 ] (see [ 16 , p.409] , [ 15 , pp.253, 255, 444] ). Lemma 3.1 . Let ν \nu be a non-negative and non-decreasing function on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} satisfying ( 2.12 ). Then (1) ∃ lim t → − ∞ ν ⁡ ( t ) = : ν ⁡ ( − ∞ ) ≥ 0 \exists\,\lim_{t\to-\infty}\nu(t)=:\nu(-\infty)\geq 0 ; (2) ∃ lim t → + ∞ ν ⁡ ( t ) / t = 0 \exists\,\lim_{t\to+\infty}\nu(t)/t=0 ; (3) ( 2.15 ) holds. Proof. Statement (1) is trivial. To prove (2) suppose on the contrary that 2 ​ a := lim ¯ t → + ∞ ν ​ ( t ) / t > 0 . 2a:=\limsp\limits_{t\to+\infty}\nu(t)/t>0\,. Then there exists a sequence of monotonically increasing positive numbers 1 < t 1 < t 2 < ⋯ < t n < t n + 1 < … , t n → + ∞ , n → ∞ , 1<t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots<t_{n}<t_{n+1}<\dots\,,\quad t_{n}\to+\infty\,,\quad n\to\infty\,, such that ν ⁡ ( t n ) t n ≥ a , n ≥ 1 . \frac{\nu(t_{n})}{t_{n}}\geq a\,,\quad n\geq 1\,. Whence we have ∑ n = 1 ∞ θ n < + ∞ , θ n := 1 − t n t n + 1 ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) , n ≥ 1 . \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\theta_{n}<+\infty\,,\quad\theta_{n}:=1-\frac{t_{n}}{t_{n+1}}\in(0,1)\,,\quad n\geq 1\,. Thus, lim n → ∞ θ n = 0 \lim_{n\to\infty}\theta_{n}=0 and without loss of generality we may assume that θ n ≤ 1 2 \theta_{n}\leq\frac{1}{2} for all n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 . Therefore t n + 1 ≤ t 1 ⋅ e 2 ​ ∑ k = 1 ∞ θ k , t_{n+1}\leq t_{1}\cdot e^{{{\mbox{\footnotesize{$2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\theta_{k}$}}}}}\,, in contradiction to the fact that t n → + ∞ t_{n}\to+\infty as n → ∞ n\to\infty , and (2) is verified. Particular case of the generalized Hölder inequality In order to prove Theorem 2.4 we need to use a special case of the generalized Hölder inequality established in [ 26 , (11), p.460] . Let ℕ := { 1 , 2 , … } {\mathbb{N}}:=\{1,2,\dots\} , ℝ + := [ 0 , + ∞ ) {\mathbb{R}}^{+}:=[0,+\infty) , A × B ⊂ ℝ 2 A\times B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2} the Cartesian product of A , B ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℝ ) A,B\in\mathcal{B}({\mathbb{R}}) [ 32 , p.8] and μ 1 × μ 2 \mu_{1}\times\mu_{2} the product of μ 1 , μ 2 ∈ ℳ + ​ ( ℝ ) \mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}) [ 32 , p.304] . Thus, for each n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} and x ∈ Q x\in Q , log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) \log\Phi_{n}(x,t) , log ⁡ φ n ​ ( t ) ∈ L 1 ​ ( Ω , d ​ P ​ ( t ) ) \log\varphi_{n}(t)\in L_{1}(\Omega,\,dP(t)) and hence it is possible to define the finite-valued functions h n ​ ( x , t ) \displaystyle h_{n}(x,t) : = log Φ n ( x , t ) − log ∫ Q Φ n ( u , t ) d ω ( u ) , \displaystyle:=\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)-\log\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)\,, H n ​ ( x ) \displaystyle H_{n}(x) : = ∫ Ω h n ( x , t ) d P ( t ) = ∫ Ω log Φ n ( x , t ) d P ( t ) − ∫ Ω ( log ∫ Q Φ n ( u , t ) d ω ( u ) ) d P ( t ) , \displaystyle:=\int_{\Omega}h_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)=\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)-\int_{\Omega}\left(\log\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)\right)\,dP(t)\,, for all t ∈ Ω t\in\Omega , x ∈ Q x\in Q and n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} . Jensen’s inequality [ 14 , Lem.6.1, p.34] applied to the exponential function gives for all x ∈ Q x\in Q and n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} , exp ⁡ ( ∫ Ω h n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ) \displaystyle\exp\left(\int_{\Omega}h_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)\right) ≤ ∫ Ω exp ⁡ ( h n ​ ( x , t ) ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) \displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\exp\left(h_{n}(x,t)\right)\,dP(t) = ∫ Ω exp ( log Φ n ( x , t ) − log ∫ Q Φ n ( u , t ) d ω ( u ) ) d P ( t ) \displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\exp\left(\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)-\log\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)\right)\,dP(t) = ∫ Ω [ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ∫ Q Φ n ​ ( u , t ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( u ) ] ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) \displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left[\frac{\Phi_{n}(x,t)}{\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\;d\omega(u)}\right]\,dP(t) = ∫ Ω [ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) φ n ​ ( t ) ] ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) , \displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left[\frac{\Phi_{n}(x,t)}{\varphi_{n}(t)}\right]\,dP(t)\,, i.e., e H n ​ ( x ) ≤ ∫ Ω [ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ∫ Q Φ n ​ ( u , t ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( u ) ] ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) = ∫ Ω [ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) φ n ​ ( t ) ] ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) , x ∈ Q , n ∈ ℕ , e^{H_{n}(x)}\leq\int_{\Omega}\left[\frac{\Phi_{n}(x,t)}{\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)}\right]\,dP(t)=\int_{\Omega}\left[\frac{\Phi_{n}(x,t)}{\varphi_{n}(t)}\right]\,dP(t)\,,\quad x\in Q\,,\ n\in{\mathbb{N}}\,, (3.8) where the function Φ n ​ ( x , t ) / φ n ​ ( t ) \Phi_{n}(x,t)/\varphi_{n}(t) , being positive and bounded above by n / γ n/\gamma on Q × Ω Q\times\Omega , belongs to L 1 ​ ( Q × Ω , d ⁡ ( ω × P ) ​ ( x , t ) ) L_{1}(Q\times\Omega,d(\omega\times P)(x,t)) . Integrating ( 3.8 ) over x ∈ Q x\in Q with respect to the measure ω \omega we obtain by Fubini’s theorem [ 32 , p.307] ∫ Q e H n ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( x ) ≤ ∫ Q ∫ Ω Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ∫ Q Φ n ​ ( u , t ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( x ) = ∫ Ω ∫ Q Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( x ) ∫ Q Φ n ​ ( u , t ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) = 1 , \int_{Q}e^{H_{n}(x)}\,d\omega(x)\leq\int_{Q}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\Phi_{n}(x,t)}{\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)}\;dP(t)\,d\omega(x)=\int_{\Omega}\dfrac{\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,d\omega(x)}{\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)}\;dP(t)=1\,, from which it follows that ∫ Q exp { ∫ Ω log Φ n ( x , t ) d P ( t ) − ∫ Ω ( log ∫ Q Φ n ( u , t ) d ω ( u ) ) d P ( t ) } d ω ( x ) ≤ 1 , \int_{Q}\exp\left\{\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)-\int_{\Omega}\left(\log\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)\right)\,dP(t)\right\}\,d\omega(x)\leq 1\,, i.e., ∫ Q exp { ∫ Ω log Φ n ( x , t ) d P ( t ) } d ω ( x ) ≤ exp { ∫ Ω ( log ∫ Q Φ n ( u , t ) d ω ( u ) ) d P ( t ) } . \int_{Q}\exp\left\{\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)\right\}\;d\omega(x)\leq\exp\left\{\int_{\Omega}\left(\log\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\;d\omega(u)\right)\,dP(t)\right\}\,. Applying Jensen’s inequality to the right-hand side of the last inequality we obtain ∫ Q exp \displaystyle\int_{Q}\exp { ∫ Ω log Φ n ( x , t ) d P ( t ) } d ω ( x ) ≤ ∫ Ω exp ( log ∫ Q Φ n ( u , t ) d ω ( u ) ) d P ( t ) \displaystyle\left\{\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)\right\}\,d\omega(x)\leq\int_{\Omega}\exp\left(\log\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)\right)dP(t) = ∫ Ω ( ∫ Q Φ n ​ ( u , t ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( u ) ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ≤ ∫ Ω ( ∫ Q Φ ⁡ ( u , t ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( u ) ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) \displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{Q}\Phi_{n}(u,t)\,d\omega(u)\right)\,dP(t)\leq\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{Q}\Phi(u,t)\,d\omega(u)\right)\,dP(t) = ∫ Ω ( ∫ Q Ψ ​ ( u , t ) ρ ⁡ ( Ω ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( u ) ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ≤ ∫ Ω D Ψ ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) = D Ψ . \displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{Q}\Psi(u,t)^{\rho(\Omega)}\,d\omega(u)\right)\,dP(t)\leq\int_{\Omega}D_{\Psi}\,dP(t)=D_{\Psi}\,. Summarizing, we have obtained the upper bound ∫ Ω exp ⁡ ( ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ) ​ 𝑑 ω ​ ( x ) ≤ D Ψ , n ∈ ℕ . \int_{\Omega}\exp\left(\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\;dP(t)\right)\,d\omega(x)\leq D_{\Psi}\,,\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}}\,. (3.9) Inequalities 0 < γ ⁡ ( x ) ≤ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ≤ Φ m ​ ( x , t ) ≤ m 0<\gamma(x)\leq\Phi_{n}(x,t)\leq\Phi_{m}(x,t)\leq m , which are valid for all ( x , t ) ∈ Q × Ω (x,t)\in Q\times\Omega and m ≥ n ≥ 1 m\geq n\geq 1 , yield that Φ n ​ ( x , t ) \Phi_{n}(x,t) , log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) \log\Phi_{n}(x,t) and exp ⁡ ( ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ) \exp\big(\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)\big) , n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} , are pointwise monotone non-decreasing sequences of finite-valued measurable functions. Hence, their limits as n → ∞ n\to\infty exist, regardless of whether they are finite or equal to + ∞ +\infty . Furthermore, the first two of these limits equal to Φ ⁡ ( x , t ) \Phi(x,t) and log ⁡ Φ ⁡ ( x , t ) \log\Phi(x,t) , respectively. Now let us define: ψ ⁡ ( x ) := lim n → ∞ ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) , x ∈ Q . \psi(x):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)\,,\quad x\in Q\,. It is clear that for each x ∈ Q x\in Q − ∞ < log ⁡ γ ⁡ ( x ) = ∫ Ω log ⁡ γ ⁡ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ≤ ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ≤ lim n → ∞ ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) = ψ ⁡ ( x ) . -\infty<\log\gamma(x)=\int_{\Omega}\log\gamma(x)\,dP(t)\leq\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\;dP(t)\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\;dP(t)=\psi(x)\,. (3.10) Let Q ψ ∞ := { x ∈ Q ∣ ψ ⁡ ( x ) = + ∞ } Q_{\psi}^{\infty}:=\big\{x\in Q\mid\psi(x)=+\infty\big\} and Q ^ := Q ∖ Q ψ ∞ \widehat{Q}:=Q\setminus Q_{\psi}^{\infty} . Then ( 3.10 ) implies that ω ⁡ ( Q ψ ∞ ) = 0 \omega\left(Q_{\psi}^{\infty}\right)=0 . Now, for each fixed x ∈ Q ^ x\in\widehat{Q} one can again apply Beppo Levi’s theorem [ 23 , p.305] to the non-decreasing sequence of integrable functions n ≥ log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , ⋅ ) > log ⁡ γ ⁡ ( x ) > − ∞ n\geq\log\Phi_{n}(x,\cdot)>\log\gamma(x)>-\infty , n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} , satisfying − ∞ < log ⁡ γ ⁡ ( x ) ≤ ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) ≤ ψ ⁡ ( x ) < + ∞ , -\infty<\log\gamma(x)\leq\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\;dP(t)\leq\psi(x)<+\infty\,, to get log ⁡ Φ ⁡ ( x , t ) ∈ L 1 ​ ( Ω , d ​ P ​ ( t ) ) \log\Phi(x,t)\in L_{1}(\Omega,dP(t)) , ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ ⁡ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) = ∫ Ω lim n → ∞ log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) = lim n → ∞ ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ n ​ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) = ψ ⁡ ( x ) < + ∞ , \int_{\Omega}\log\Phi(x,t)\,dP(t)=\int_{\Omega}\lim_{n\to\infty}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi_{n}(x,t)\,dP(t)=\psi(x)<+\infty\;, and hence, log ⁡ Ψ ⁡ ( x , t ) ∈ L 1 ​ ( Ω , d ​ ρ ​ ( t ) ) \log\Psi(x,t)\in L_{1}(\Omega,d\rho(t)) . Thus, ψ ⁡ ( x ) = ∫ Ω log ⁡ Φ ⁡ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 P ​ ( t ) = ∫ Ω log ⁡ Ψ ⁡ ( x , t ) ​ 𝑑 ρ ​ ( t ) . \psi(x)=\int_{\Omega}\log\Phi(x,t)\,dP(t)=\int_{\Omega}\log\Psi(x,t)\,d\rho(t)\,. Substitution of the last expression for ψ \psi in ( 3.10 ) proves ( 3.7 ) and completes the proof of Lemma 3.2 . ∎ 4. Proofs of the main assertions 4.1. Proof of Theorem 2.1 4.1.1. Proof of item 1. (4.8) But σ ⁡ ( x ) \sigma(x) , being a nondecreasing function on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} , is bounded above by σ ⁡ ( A ) \sigma(A) in ( − ∞ , A ] (-\infty,A] for every A > 0 A>0 . Thus, in view of [ 17 , 111, p.91] , the property ( 4.8 ) means that σ ⁡ ( x ) \sigma(x) is convex i.e., σ ⁡ ( θ ​ x 1 + ( 1 − θ ) ​ x 2 ) ≤ θ ​ σ ​ ( x 1 ) + ( 1 − θ ) ​ σ ​ ( x 2 ) ∀ x 1 , x 2 ∈ ℝ ∀ θ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] . \sigma\left(\theta x_{1}+(1-\theta)x_{2}\right)\leq\theta\sigma(x_{1})+(1-\theta)\sigma(x_{2})\qquad\forall\,x_{1},x_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,1]\,. Then, on each segment [ − A , A ] [-A,A] , A > 0 A>0 , the function σ \sigma satisfies a Lipschitz condition and is thus absolutely continuous (see [ 29 , Th.1, p.230] , [ 28 , p.244] ). Furthermore, by [ 17 , 111, p.91] , the one-sided derivatives of σ ⁡ ( x ) \sigma(x) exist at every point x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} ν ⁡ ( x + 0 ) := lim h ↓ 0 σ ⁡ ( x + h ) − σ ⁡ ( x ) h , ν ⁡ ( x − 0 ) := lim h ↓ 0 σ ⁡ ( x ) − σ ⁡ ( x − h ) h , \nu(x+0):=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}{{\mbox{\large{$\frac{\sigma(x+h)-\sigma(x)}{\vphantom{A^{A^{a}}}h}$}}}}\,,\qquad\nu(x-0):=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}{{\mbox{\large{$\frac{\sigma(x)-\sigma(x-h)}{\vphantom{A^{A^{a}}}h}$}}}}\,, (4.9) and moreover, since the function σ \sigma is convex and nondecreasing on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} , its derivative ν \nu satisfies: 0 ≤ ν ⁡ ( x − 0 ) ≤ ν ⁡ ( x + 0 ) ≤ ν ⁡ ( y − 0 ) ≤ ν ⁡ ( y + 0 ) for − ∞ < x < y < + ∞ . 0\leq\nu(x-0)\leq\nu(x+0)\leq\nu(y-0)\leq\nu(y+0)\quad\text{for}\quad-\infty<x<y<+\infty\,. Putting ν ⁡ ( x ) \nu(x) to be an arbitrary number in the interval [ ν ⁡ ( x − 0 ) , ν ⁡ ( x + 0 ) CLOSE [\nu(x-0),\nu(x+0) , x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} , we get a non-negative nondecreasing function ν \nu on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} which according to [ 27 , Prop.4.9, p.202; (4.27), p.203] satisfies σ ⁡ ( E ) = ∫ E ν ⁡ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x , for any bounded Borel set E ⊂ ℝ . \sigma(E)=\int_{E}\nu(x)\,dx\,,\quad\text{for any bounded Borel set $E\subset{\mathbb{R}}$}\,. The change of variable theorem [ 27 , Cor 4.3, p.214] applied to ( 4.1 ) and ( 4.2 ), gives ( 2.9 ) and ( 2.10 ), respectively. We now prove the inverse statement. For any x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} and h > 0 h>0 the property α ​ y + ∫ − ∞ + ∞ y y 2 + ( x − t ) 2 ​ ν ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t ≤ α ​ y + ∫ − ∞ + ∞ y y 2 + ( x + h − t ) 2 ​ ν ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t , \alpha y+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{y}{y^{2}+(x-t)^{2}}\ \nu(t)\ dt\leq\alpha y+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{y}{y^{2}+(x+h-t)^{2}}\ \nu(t)\ dt\ , or, what is the same, ∫ − ∞ + ∞ y y 2 + ( x − t ) 2 ​ ν ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t ≤ ∫ − ∞ + ∞ y y 2 + ( x + h − t ) 2 ​ ν ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t = ∫ − ∞ + ∞ y y 2 + ( x − t ) 2 ​ ν ​ ( t + h ) ​ 𝑑 t , \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{y}{y^{2}+(x-t)^{2}}\ \nu(t)\ dt\leq\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{y}{y^{2}+(x+h-t)^{2}}\ \nu(t)\ dt=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{y}{y^{2}+(x-t)^{2}}\ \nu(t+h)\ dt\ , follows from the evident inequality ∫ − ∞ + ∞ y y 2 + ( x − t ) 2 ​ ( ν ⁡ ( t + h ) − ν ⁡ ( t ) ) ​ 𝑑 t ≥ 0 . \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{y}{y^{2}+(x-t)^{2}}\ (\nu(t+h)-\nu(t))\ dt\geq 0\ . Since ( 4.2 ) is unique (see [ 10 , Th.II, p.20] ) and the Radon-Nikodym derivative ν = d ​ σ / d ​ m \nu=d\sigma/dm is determined almost everywhere on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} we have the uniqueness of ( 2.10 ) provided that all functions in the class ℵ ν \aleph_{\,\nu} , defined in ( 2.4 ), are identified. 4.1.2. Proof of item 2. Note first that any function f ∈ 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 f\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P} belongs to the intersectio of the classes 𝒫 ∫ ∩ 𝒫 {\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\mathcal{P} and log ⁡ 𝒫 \log\mathcal{P} . The proof is complete. 4.7. Proofs of Theorem 2.6 and Corollary 2.3 4.7.1. Proof of Theorem 2.6 . Observe that the first item of Theorem 2.6 follows directly from Theorem 2.3 and ( 1.17 ). If φ ∈ 𝒫 log ∖ exp ⁡ Δ 𝒫 ∫ \varphi\in\mathcal{P}_{\log}\setminus\exp{\Delta_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}}} then φ = e f \varphi=e^{f} , where f ∈ ( 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 ) ∖ Δ 𝒫 ∫ f\in\left({\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P}\right)\setminus\Delta_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}} . Using the notation of Theorems 2.3 and 2.5 we can apply the results of Theorem 2.4 to the function f f in order to get with the help of the relations ( 4.30 ) and ( 4.31 ) the validity of all statements of Theorem 2.6 except ( 2.36 ). Equality ( 2.36 ) holds true due to the well-known consequence of M.Riesz’s theorem [ 24 , p.128] . 4.7.2. Proof of Corollary 2.3 . If φ ⁡ ( z ) = e f \varphi(z)=e^{f} , where f = ∫ 0 1 log ⁡ 1 1 − t ​ z ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( t ) f=\int_{0}^{1}\log\frac{1}{1-tz}\,d\mu(t) , then by Corollary 2.2 f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0 , f ∈ Hol ⁡ ( ( , , , ) ) f\in\hol((-\infty,1)) and f ∈ 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 f\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P} , which imply the validity of all statements of Corollary 2.3 . Conversely, assume that φ ⁡ ( 0 ) = 1 \varphi(0)=1 , φ ∈ Hol ⁡ ( ( , , , ) ) \varphi\in\hol((-\infty,1)) and φ ∈ 𝒫 log \varphi\in\mathcal{P}_{\log} . Then there exists f ∈ 𝒫 ∫ ∩ log ⁡ 𝒫 f\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\int}\cap\log\mathcal{P} such that φ ⁡ ( z ) = e f \varphi(z)=e^{f} and f ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 f(0)=0 . Since φ ∈ 𝒫 \varphi\in\mathcal{P} and φ ∈ Hol ⁡ ( ( , , , ) ) \varphi\in\hol((-\infty,1)) the property ( 1.4 ) and representation ( 1.3 ) imply that Im ⁡ φ ⁡ ( x + i0 ) = 0 \IM\varphi(x+i0)=0 for any x < 1 x<1 . Then it follows from ( 2.3 ) that the function ν \nu corresponding to φ \varphi by the exponential representation ( 2.34 ) satisfies sin ⁡ π ​ ν ​ ( x ) = 0 \sin\pi\nu(x)=0 for almost all x < 1 x<1 . Since by Theorem 2.6 ν \nu is a nondecreasing function on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} with values in [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] then for almost all x < 1 x<1 either ν ⁡ ( x ) = 0 \nu(x)=0 or ν ⁡ ( x ) = 1 \nu(x)=1 or ν ( x ) = χ [ a , + ∞ ) ( x ) \nu(x)=\chi_{[a,+\infty)}(x) for some a < 1 a<1 . Assume that ν ⁡ ( x ) = 1 \nu(x)=1 for almost all x < 1 x<1 . Then ν ⁡ ( x ) ≡ 1 \nu(x)\equiv 1 on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} and in view of ( 2.34 ), φ ⁡ ( z ) \varphi(z) is a constant function and so f f is constant as well. By Corollary 2.2 f ≡ 0 f\equiv 0 and f f can be represented in the form ( 2.26 ), from which ( 2.39 ) follows. If for almost all x < 1 x<1 , ν ( x ) = χ [ a , + ∞ ) ( x ) \nu(x)=\chi_{[a,+\infty)}(x) for some a < 1 a<1 then ν ( x ) ≡ χ [ a , + ∞ ) ( x ) \nu(x)\equiv\chi_{[a,+\infty)}(x) on ℝ ∖ { a } {\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{a\} and it follows from ( 2.34 ) and φ ⁡ ( 0 ) = 1 \varphi(0)=1 that φ ⁡ ( z ) = a / ( a − z ) \varphi(z)=a/(a-z) , z ∈ ℍ z\in{\mathbb{H}} . By ( 1.2 ) this means that φ ⁡ ( z ) = a / ( a − z ) \varphi(z)=a/(a-z) , z ∈ ℂ z\in{\mathbb{C}} , which contradicts φ ∈ Hol ⁡ ( ( , , , ) ) \varphi\in\hol((-\infty,1)) . Finally, ν ⁡ ( x ) = 0 \nu(x)=0 for almost all x < 1 x<1 implies ν ⁡ ( x ) = 0 \nu(x)=0 for all x < 1 x<1 . Due to the formulas ( 4.12 ) and ( 4.15 ), the function f f can be represented in the form ( 2.26 ) with μ \mu given by ( 4.13 ), which means the validity of the representation ( 2.39 ). The proof of Corollary 2.3 is complete. 4.8. Proof of Theorem 2.7 4.8.1. Necessity. Proof of Theorem 2.8 Observe first that if v v satisfies ( 2.46a ) inequality ( 2.46c ) holds for arbitrary − ∞ < x 1 < x 2 < 1 -\infty<x_{1}<x_{2}<1 and − ∞ < x 1 < 1 < x 2 < + ∞ -\infty<x_{1}<1<x_{2}<+\infty . Let Ψ \Psi be universally starlike and φ ⁡ ( z ) := Ψ ⁡ ( z ) / z \varphi(z):=\Psi(z)/z . In this case, due to Theorem 2.7 , we can find p > 1 p>1 and v ∈ L p ​ ( ℝ , d ​ x ) v\in L_{p}({\mathbb{R}},dx) such that the representation ( 2.47 ) holds, v ⁡ ( x ) ≥ 0 v(x)\geq 0 for all x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} , v v satisfies ( 2.46c ) and in view of ( 2.42 ), φ ⁡ ( x + i ​ 0 ) = v ~ ​ ( x ) + i ​ v ​ ( x ) \varphi(x+i0)=\widetilde{v}(x)+iv(x) m m -a.e. on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} .
